Here $h=2/5$, and both jets are imposed at every positive node of the periodic superset. The common energy conclusion therefore does not identify the two auxiliary-function theorems.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The sine product and its node coordinates

<a id="eq:periodic-coefficients"></a>
We now begin the construction of the theorem’s pair. The first ingredient is a function with a double zero at every prescribed node. Define <a id="eq:periodic-polynomial"></a>$$\label{eq:periodic-polynomial}
 P(s)=\prod_{a\in L}\left(2\sin\frac{\pi(s-a)}{12}\right)^2
     =\sum_{l=-6}^{6}P_l e^{i\pi t_l s},\qquad t_l=\frac l6.$$ This is an entire trigonometric polynomial, nonnegative and 12-periodic on the real axis, with a double zero at each point of $\mathcal Z$ and no other real zeros. Its Fourier coefficients are $$\label{eq:periodic-coefficients}
 (P_0,P_1,\ldots,P_6)
 =\left(5,-1+2ib,1+2ib,-2,-\frac12+ib,-1-2ib,1\right),
 \qquad P_{-l}=\overline{P_l}.$$ Indeed, setting $w=e^{i\pi s/6}$ expresses the product as $w^{-6}\prod_{a\in L}(w-e^{i\pi a/6})^2$. The scalar factor is one because $\sum_{a\in L}a=24$; multiplying this degree-twelve polynomial gives <a href="#eq:periodic-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:periodic-coefficients">[eq:periodic-coefficients]</a>.

<a id="eq:node-expansion"></a>
<a id="eq:node-constants"></a>
<a id="eq:node-constant-bounds"></a>
At a node $n\in\mathcal N_0$, write $$\label{eq:node-expansion}
 P(n+u)=Q_nu^2\bigl(1+D_nu+O(u^2)\bigr).$$ The periodicity of $P$ makes $Q_n,D_n$ depend only on the residue $a$ of $n$: $$\label{eq:node-constants}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 a&Q_n&D_n\\ \hline
 0&\pi^2/3&-7\pi\sqrt3/18\\
 1&(2\pi^2/3)(2-\sqrt3)&\pi(3+\sqrt3)/18\\
 3&(2\pi^2/3)(2-\sqrt3)&-\pi(3+\sqrt3)/18\\
 4&\pi^2/3&7\pi\sqrt3/18\\
 7&(2\pi^2/3)(2+\sqrt3)&-\pi(3-\sqrt3)/18\\
 9&(2\pi^2/3)(2+\sqrt3)&\pi(3-\sqrt3)/18
\end{array}$$ To obtain the table, remove the vanishing sine factor and take the value and logarithmic derivative of the remaining product: $$Q_a=\left(\frac\pi6\right)^2
       \prod_{d\in L\setminus\{a\}}
        \left(2\sin\frac{\pi(a-d)}{12}\right)^2,
 \qquad
 D_a=\frac\pi6\sum_{d\in L\setminus\{a\}}
             \cot\frac{\pi(a-d)}{12}.$$ The angle-addition formulas give the displayed entries. In particular, $$\label{eq:node-constant-bounds}
 Q_n>\frac74=1.75,\qquad |D_n|<\frac{53}{25}=2.12.$$ For these strict comparisons, $157/50<\pi<22/7$ and $\sqrt3<1733/1000$ suffice. The smallest $Q_n$ is greater than $(2/3)(157/50)^2(267/1000)>7/4$, while the largest $|D_n|$ is $7\pi\sqrt3/18<(22/18)(1733/1000)<53/25$. The upper bound for $\pi$ is proved in Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a>; the lower bound for $\pi$ and the upper bound for $\sqrt3$ are proved in Appendix <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">9</a>.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A spectral realization of summable cardinal data

Double-pole value terms and simple-pole derivative terms are familiar from the one-dimensional constructions of Cohn and Kumar (Cohn and Kumar 2007, Proposition 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[CohnKumar2007](#ref-CohnKumar2007)] and Carneiro et al. (Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler 2013, secs. 1, (1.2)–(1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013](#ref-CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013)]. Here we need their planar Fourier transform and bounds uniform in the node location. A compactly supported spectral measure provides both.

<a id="eq:cardinal-function"></a>
A coefficient list consists of a real constant $C$ and real pairs $(c_n,d_n)$ for $n\in\mathcal N_0$, with the unweighted norm $$\|a\|_1:=|C|+\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_0}(|c_n|+|d_n|)<\infty.$$ The letter $a$ denotes the full list. Away from the nodes, put $$\label{eq:cardinal-function}
 p_a(s)=P(s)\left(C+
       \sum_{n\in\mathcal N_0}
          \left(\frac{c_n}{(s-n)^2}+\frac{d_n}{s-n}\right)\right).$$ We suppress the subscript when the list is understood.

<a id="stmt-lem-cardinal-measure"></a>
<div id="lem:cardinal-measure" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cardinal-jets"></a>
<a id="eq:full-measure"></a>
<a id="eq:spectral-variation"></a>
<a id="eq:folded-evaluation"></a>
<a id="eq:folded-density"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Cardinal measure). *The function in <a href="#eq:cardinal-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cardinal-function">[eq:cardinal-function]</a> extends to an entire function, real on the real axis, with jets $$\label{eq:cardinal-jets}
 p_a(n)=Q_nc_n,\qquad p_a'(n)=Q_n(d_n+D_nc_n)
       \quad(n\in\mathcal N_0).$$ There is a finite complex measure $\mu_a$ on $[-1,1]$ such that $$\label{eq:full-measure}
 p_a(s)=\int_{-1}^{1}e^{i\pi ts}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu_a(t)\qquad(s\in\mathbb C),$$ $\mu_a(-E)=\overline{\mu_a(E)}$ for every Borel set $E$, and $$\label{eq:spectral-variation}
 \|\mu_a\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le 25|C|+36\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_0}(|c_n|+|d_n|).$$ For real $s$, the same value can be evaluated from a folded measure on $[0,1]$: $$\label{eq:folded-evaluation}
 p_a(s)=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\int_0^1e^{i\pi ts}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\nu_a(t).$$ The atoms of $\nu_a$ are $Ce_jP_j$ at $t_j$, where $e_0=1$ and $e_j=2$ for $1\le j\le6$. Its density on the open frequency intervals is $$\label{eq:folded-density}
 d_j(t)=2\pi\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_0}e^{-i\pi tn}
       \sum_{l=j}^{6}
           \bigl[-\pi c_n(t_l-t)+id_n\bigr]P_le^{i\pi t_ln}
 \quad(t_{j-1}<t<t_j).$$ The same variation bound holds for $\nu_a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cardinal-measure"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cardinal-measure</summary>

*Proof.* First consider one node $n$. For $u=s-n$ and real $t_l$, direct integration gives the entire identities $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{e^{i\pi t_lu}-1}{u}
    &=i\pi\int_0^{t_l}e^{i\pi tu}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt,\\
 \frac{e^{i\pi t_lu}-1-i\pi t_lu}{u^2}
    &=-\pi^2\int_0^{t_l}(t_l-t)e^{i\pi tu}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt.
\end{aligned}$$ The integrals include the removable values at $u=0$. Multiply these identities by $P_le^{i\pi t_ln}$ and sum over $l$. The subtracted constant and linear terms cancel because $P(n)=P'(n)=0$. We therefore obtain spectral measures for the two entire node fractions in <a href="#eq:cardinal-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cardinal-function">[eq:cardinal-function]</a>. Their negative-frequency parts are conjugate reflections of their positive-frequency parts. Folding doubles the latter parts and gives exactly <a href="#eq:folded-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:folded-density">[eq:folded-density]</a>. These node measures are absolutely continuous; the atoms come only from $CP$, with the zero frequency counted once and every positive frequency counted twice.

The coefficient row in <a href="#eq:periodic-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:periodic-coefficients">[eq:periodic-coefficients]</a> gives $(|P_1|,\ldots,|P_6|)=(2,2,2,1,2,1)$. The constant column consequently has variation $|P_0|+2\sum_{l=1}^6|P_l|=25$. Taking absolute values in the two integrals above gives, per unit $c_n$ and $d_n$, respectively, $$\pi^2\sum_{l=1}^6t_l^2|P_l|=\frac{65\pi^2}{18}<36,
 \qquad
 2\pi\sum_{l=1}^6t_l|P_l|=\frac{32\pi}{3}<36.$$ The phases involving $n$ have modulus one at real frequencies, so these bounds are independent of the node. They apply to the full measure and to its folded form.

For an absolutely summable list, the node measures now converge in total variation, proving <a href="#eq:spectral-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectral-variation">[eq:spectral-variation]</a>. On every compact subset of the complex $s$-plane, $e^{i\pi ts}$ and each of its $s$-derivatives are uniformly bounded for $|t|\le1$. Thus the measure integral and all its derivatives are locally uniform limits of those for finite lists. On a compact set avoiding the nodes, the reciprocal factors in the cardinal sum are uniformly bounded over all $n$, so absolute summability also gives uniform convergence of that sum there. The integral thus defines its entire extension, with the stated removals.

For a finite list, <a href="#eq:node-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:node-expansion">[eq:node-expansion]</a> gives <a href="#eq:cardinal-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cardinal-jets">[eq:cardinal-jets]</a>: the designated double and simple fractions give the two displayed terms, while every other term vanishes to order at least two at the node. Locally uniform convergence of the functions and their derivatives passes these identities to the full list. Finally, conjugate symmetry of the measure gives reality on the real axis and the folded formula. ◻

</details>

The entire extension is always the full complex integral <a href="#eq:full-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-measure">[eq:full-measure]</a>. The real part in <a href="#eq:folded-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:folded-evaluation">[eq:folded-evaluation]</a> is an evaluation rule for real $s$, not a definition at complex arguments.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Gaussian damping and the transformed kernel

<a id="eq:transformed-kernel"></a>
For $-1\le t\le1$, set $$\label{eq:transformed-kernel}
 z(t)=-\frac{B}{t+ih}-ih,\qquad
 \lambda(t)=\frac{i}{b(t+ih)}.$$ For a coefficient list $a$, define <a id="eq:transformed-function"></a>$$\label{eq:transformed-function}
 K_{p_a}(s)=\int_{-1}^1\lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)s}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu_a(t).$$ The image of $[-1,1]$ under $z$ is compact and $\lambda$ is bounded there, so the same compact-uniform argument makes $K_{p_a}$ entire. Also, $$\lambda(-t)=\overline{\lambda(t)},\qquad
 z(-t)=-\overline{z(t)}.$$ It follows that $K_{p_a}$ is real on the real axis, where <a id="eq:folded-transformed-function"></a>$$\label{eq:folded-transformed-function}
 K_{p_a}(s)=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\int_0^1
                   \lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)s}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\nu_a(t).$$

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 4* (Pointwise frequency consistency). The rational formulas in <a href="#eq:transformed-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transformed-kernel">[eq:transformed-kernel]</a> also make sense for complex $t\ne-ih$. Since $z(t)+ih=-B/(t+ih)$, the image avoids $-ih$, and substitution twice gives $$z(z(t))=t,\qquad
 \lambda(t)\lambda(z(t))
 =\frac{i}{b(t+ih)}\frac{-i(t+ih)}{bB}=1.$$ These are pointwise frequency and amplitude identities for the Gaussian transform. They do not define a composition of the folded real-contour integral: $z$ does not preserve that contour, and a transformed profile has not thereby been given a cardinal measure on $[-1,1]$. The involution for the radial functions follows from the genuine Fourier transform below.

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-fourier-pair"></a>
<div id="lem:fourier-pair" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:coupled-pair"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Fourier pairs). *For every coefficient list $a$, the radial functions $e^{-\pi hs}p_a(s)$ and $e^{-\pi hs}K_{p_a}(s)$ are real Schwartz functions on $\mathbb R^2$ and are Fourier transforms of one another. Consequently, for any two lists, the entire functions $$\label{eq:coupled-pair}
 H_1=p_1+K_{p_2},\qquad H_2=p_2+K_{p_1}$$ give the real radial Schwartz Fourier pair $$f(x)=e^{-\pi hb|x|^2}H_1(b|x|^2),\qquad
 \widehat f(\xi)=e^{-\pi hb|\xi|^2}H_2(b|\xi|^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fourier-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fourier-pair</summary>

<a id="eq:complex-gaussian-transform"></a>
*Proof.* For a complex parameter $\gamma$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\gamma>0$, the Gaussian transform in dimension two, with kernel $e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}$, is $$\label{eq:complex-gaussian-transform}
 \widehat{e^{-\pi\gamma|x|^2}}(\xi)
       =\gamma^{-1}e^{-\pi|\xi|^2/\gamma}.$$ For real $\gamma>0$, squaring the Gaussian integral and using polar coordinates gives the one-dimensional value $\gamma^{-1/2}$ at frequency zero. Integration by parts shows that the one-dimensional transform obeys $F'(\xi)=-(2\pi\xi/\gamma)F(\xi)$. Solving this differential equation and taking the product in two coordinates proves <a href="#eq:complex-gaussian-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complex-gaussian-transform">[eq:complex-gaussian-transform]</a> for positive real $\gamma$. Both sides are holomorphic on the right half-plane; differentiation under the integral is locally justified by a polynomial times a decaying Gaussian. The identity theorem gives the complex case.

After multiplication by $e^{-\pi hs}$, the integrand in <a href="#eq:full-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-measure">[eq:full-measure]</a> is a Gaussian with parameter $\gamma=b(h-it)=-ib(t+ih)$, whose real part is the fixed positive number $bh$. Using $B=1/b^2$, its transform is $$\frac1{b(h-it)}
    \exp\left(-\frac{\pi|\xi|^2}{b(h-it)}\right)
 =e^{-\pi hs}\lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)s},\qquad s=b|\xi|^2.$$ Integrating this identity against $\mu_a$ proves the forward Fourier formula. The exchanges with Fourier integration, derivatives, and polynomial weights are justified uniformly: the measure is finite, the frequency interval is compact, and every differentiated real-space Gaussian is bounded by a fixed polynomial times a decaying Gaussian. On the transformed side, the real part of the Gaussian parameter is $h/[b(h^2+t^2)]$, with a positive lower bound on $[-1,1]$. The same domination applies there. Both functions are therefore Schwartz.

Fourier transformation twice reflects a Schwartz function. The functions here are radial, so reflection fixes them and proves the reverse transform. Linearity gives <a href="#eq:coupled-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coupled-pair">[eq:coupled-pair]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The jet operator and its distant outputs

<a id="eq:jet-projection"></a>
<a id="eq:jet-operator"></a>
For a function differentiable at the positive nodes, define the jet projection $$\label{eq:jet-projection}
 (\mathcal Ju)_{c,n}=\frac{u(n)}{Q_n},\qquad
 (\mathcal Ju)_{d,n}=\frac{u'(n)-D_nu(n)}{Q_n}
       \quad(n\in\mathcal N).$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:cardinal-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cardinal-measure">3</a>, $\mathcal Jp_a$ is precisely the positive-node part of $a$. The three remaining coordinates, in the order $(c_0,d_0,C)$, are called the extra coordinates. They make no direct contribution to the jets at positive nodes; we will choose them in Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a>. Put $$\label{eq:jet-operator}
 Sa=-\mathcal J K_{p_a}.$$ For two full lists $a_1,a_2$, let $a_i^+$ denote their positive-node parts. The coupled pair <a href="#eq:coupled-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coupled-pair">[eq:coupled-pair]</a> then satisfies $$\mathcal JH_1=a_1^+-Sa_2,\qquad
 \mathcal JH_2=a_2^+-Sa_1.$$ Thus the interpolation problem asks for these two expressions to equal $\mathcal JT_k$ and zero, respectively. This explains the minus sign in the definition of $S$. The next estimates bound the transformed contribution, especially at distant output nodes, uniformly in the locations of the input coefficients.

The same letter $S$ denotes its restriction to positive-node inputs with zero extras. Subscripts specify input and output restrictions. All list norms are unweighted sums of absolute values, including the sum of the norms on a pair of lists; operator norms are induced by them. A finite matrix therefore has the maximum absolute column-sum norm.

<a id="stmt-lem-kernel-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:kernel-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:last-interval-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:tail-bound"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Kernel and tail bounds). *For $0\le t\le1$, <a id="eq:global-kernel-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:global-kernel-bounds}
 |\lambda(t)|<3,\qquad |z(t)|\le\frac{44}{15}<3,
 \qquad \pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)>.187.$$ The last quantity exceeds $.70$ when $0\le t\le5/6$. On $5/6\le t\le1$, the sharper bounds are $$\label{eq:last-interval-bounds}
 |\lambda(t)|<1.25,\qquad |z(t)|<1.32,
 \qquad \pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)>.187+2.48(1-t).$$ The operator $S$, with extra input coordinates included if desired, maps coefficient lists boundedly to positive-node $\ell^1$ lists and has norm less than 4000. More precisely, if $$w\ge\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_0}(|c_n|+|d_n|),\qquad c\ge|C|,$$ then for every positive integer $r$, $$\label{eq:tail-bound}
 \sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal N\\n\ge r}}
       \bigl(|(Sa)_{c,n}|+|(Sa)_{d,n}|\bigr)
 \le U_r(w,c):=
 800(w+c)\frac{e^{-.70r}}{1-e^{-.70}}
 +5.3\left(\frac{2.54w}{r}+2c\right)
                  \frac{e^{-.187r}}{1-e^{-.187}}.$$ For several lists, the same formula bounds the sum of their tail outputs when $w,c$ bound the corresponding sums of input coefficients.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-kernel-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-kernel-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Write $q(t)=\pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)$ within this proof. Direct calculation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |\lambda(t)|&=\frac1{b\sqrt{t^2+h^2}},\\
 |z(t)|^2&=h^2+\frac{B^2-2Bh^2}{t^2+h^2},\\
 q(t)&=\pi h\left(\frac{B}{t^2+h^2}-1\right).
\end{aligned}$$ Since $B>2h^2$, all three expressions decrease for $t\ge0$. At zero, $|\lambda|=5/\sqrt3<3$ and $|z|=44/15$. At the other relevant endpoints, $$q(1)=\frac{26\pi}{435}>.187,\qquad
 q(5/6)=\frac{862\pi}{3845}>.70,$$ and $$|\lambda(5/6)|=\frac{60}{\sqrt{2307}}<\frac54,
 \qquad
 |z(5/6)|^2=\frac{33476}{19225}<\left(\frac{33}{25}\right)^2.$$ Finally, $$-q'(t)=\frac{2\pi hBt}{(t^2+h^2)^2}.$$ The function $t/(t^2+h^2)^2$ decreases for $t>h/\sqrt3$. Its value at the last endpoint therefore gives $-q'(t)\ge2000\pi/2523>2.48$ throughout $[5/6,1]$. Integrating from $t$ to 1 proves the last inequality in <a href="#eq:last-interval-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:last-interval-bounds">[eq:last-interval-bounds]</a>. All strict numerical comparisons here follow from $157/50<\pi<22/7$ and squaring the positive rational bounds.

<a id="eq:jet-kernel-cost"></a>
One unit of spectral variation at frequency $t$ contributes at most $$\label{eq:jet-kernel-cost}
 \frac{|\lambda(t)|}{Q_n}
       \bigl(1+|D_n|+\pi|z(t)|\bigr)e^{-q(t)n}$$ to the sum of the two absolute jet coordinates at $n$. By <a href="#eq:node-constant-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:node-constant-bounds">[eq:node-constant-bounds]</a>, its prefactor is less than 22 globally and less than 5.3 on the last interval, since $$\frac{3(1+2.12+3\pi)}{1.75}<22,
 \qquad
 \frac{1.25(1+2.12+1.32\pi)}{1.75}<5.3.$$ The variation bound is at most $36\|a\|_1$. Summing <a href="#eq:jet-kernel-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jet-kernel-cost">[eq:jet-kernel-cost]</a> over all positive integers, a superset of the positive nodes, therefore gives $$\|S\|\le\frac{22\cdot36}{e^{.187}-1}<4000.$$ For the final comparison, $e^{.187}-1>.187+(.187)^2/2>.198$.

For the sharper tail, split the folded measure into $[0,5/6]$ and $(5/6,1]$, assigning the atom at $5/6$ to the first part. The first part has variation at most $36w+25c$. Its contribution to the row pair at $n$ is therefore at most $$22(36w+25c)e^{-.70n}\le800(w+c)e^{-.70n}.$$ On the last open frequency interval, only $l=6$ remains in <a href="#eq:folded-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:folded-density">[eq:folded-density]</a>. Here $|P_6|=1$ and $\pi(1-t)\le\pi/6<1$, so the density has absolute value at most $2\pi w$. The only atom in $(5/6,1]$ is the one at 1, of mass $2C$. This part contributes at most $$5.3e^{-.187n}
       \left(2\pi w\int_{5/6}^1e^{-2.48n(1-t)}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt+2c\right)
 \le5.3e^{-.187n}\left(\frac{2.54w}{n}+2c\right),$$ because $2\pi/2.48<2.54$. Summing over the integers $n\ge r$ and using $1/n\le1/r$ proves <a href="#eq:tail-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-bound">[eq:tail-bound]</a>. Only absolute-value sums were used, so the same argument applies to several lists with their summed inputs. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:refined-derivative-bound"></a>
The same estimates control every derivative. If $V=25|C|+36\sum_n(|c_n|+|d_n|)$, then for every integer $j\ge0$ and real $s\ge0$, differentiation of the spectral integrals gives <a id="eq:uniform-derivative-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:uniform-derivative-bounds}
 |p_a^{(j)}(s)|\le\pi^jV,\qquad
 |K_{p_a}^{(j)}(s)|\le3(3\pi)^j e^{-.187s}V.$$ Keeping the two frequency ranges separate as above gives, for $s>0$, $$\label{eq:refined-derivative-bound}
 |K_{p_a}^{(j)}(s)|
 \le3(3\pi)^j(36w+25c)e^{-.70s}
    +1.25(1.32\pi)^j
          \left(\frac{2.54w}{s}+2c\right)e^{-.187s}.$$ In particular, the jet, tail, and derivative bounds are uniform in the input node locations and require no finite-support assumption.

<a id="sec:finite"></a>
### Positive quadrature and finite data

The spectral formulas of Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a> turn coefficient lists into genuine Fourier pairs. We now construct ten pairs of finite coefficient columns. Their linear combinations will approximate the two lists required by the coupled jet equations. The columns are fixed; only the coefficients of the combination vary with the Gaussian parameter. We control the construction in two ways: a uniform quadrature estimate bounds the error from replacing the six density integrals by finite sums, and finite interval comparisons establish the norm and sign bounds for the columns. Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">4</a> then corrects the lists to exact interpolation, and Section <a href="#sec:signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:signs">5</a> uses these bounds to prove the signs.

All terminating decimals in this section denote exact rational numbers. For a real matrix, $\left\lVert A\right\rVert$ is its maximum absolute column sum. A column has its $\ell^1$ norm, denoted $\left\lVert v\right\rVert_1$. Matrix products below are ordinary products, without complex conjugation.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A positive rule for the six density integrals

Set $$M=168,\qquad \mathcal F=\mathcal N\cap[1,M],\qquad N=384.$$ There are $84$ nodes in $\mathcal F$: each of the fourteen successive blocks of twelve positive integers contains six permitted residues. A finite positive-node list has $168$ coordinates, first all $c_n$ and then all $d_n$, with $n$ increasing in each group. We append the extra coordinates $e=(c_0,d_0,C)$ in that order. Thus the complete finite input space has $171$ coordinates.

<a id="eq:quadrature-rule"></a>
The folded spectral measure in Lemma <a href="#lem:cardinal-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cardinal-measure">3</a> has a density on each interval $((j-1)/6,j/6)$ and seven atoms at $t_j=j/6$. We leave the atoms unchanged. On each density interval define $$\label{eq:quadrature-rule}
\begin{aligned}
 \alpha_v&=\frac{v+1/2}{N},&
 \tau_{jv}&=\frac{2j-1+\cos(\pi\alpha_v)}{12},\\
 o_v&=\frac1N\left(1-2\sum_{a=1}^{N/2-1}
             \frac{\cos(2a\pi\alpha_v)}{4a^2-1}\right),
 &0&\le v<N,\quad 1\le j\le6.
\end{aligned}$$ The replacement of the density integral is the sum of masses $o_vd_j(\tau_{jv})/6$. Since $0<\pi\alpha_v<\pi$, every quadrature point lies inside its frequency interval. This is the first Fejér rule described by Waldvogel (Waldvogel 2006, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Waldvogel2006](#ref-Waldvogel2006)], divided by two so that its weights on $[-1,1]$ approximate the average rather than the integral of mass two. We prove the exactness and positivity needed for this normalization.

<a id="eq:positive-quadrature-weights"></a>
Put $\theta_v=\pi(v+1/2)/N$. For $1\le l\le2N-1$, the finite identity $$\sum_{v=0}^{N-1}\cos((v+1/2)t)=\frac{\sin(Nt)}{2\sin(t/2)}$$ at $t=l\pi/N$ gives $N^{-1}\sum_v\cos(l\theta_v)=0$. Applying the product-to-sum formula, for $1\le l\le N-1$ and $1\le a\le N/2-1$ we obtain $$\frac1N\sum_{v=0}^{N-1}\cos(l\theta_v)\cos(2a\theta_v)
 =\begin{cases}1/2,&l=2a,\\0,&l\ne2a.\end{cases}$$ Consequently $$\sum_{v=0}^{N-1}o_v\cos(l\theta_v)
 =\begin{cases}
 1,&l=0,\\
 0,&l\text{ odd},\\
 1/(1-l^2),&l>0\text{ even},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad 0\le l\le N-1.$$ Let $C_l$ be the Chebyshev polynomial defined by $C_l(\cos\theta)=\cos(l\theta)$. These values equal $\frac12\int_{-1}^1C_l(x)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx$, as follows by substituting $x=\cos\theta$. The polynomials $C_0,\ldots,C_{N-1}$ span all polynomials of degree at most $N-1$, so the rule is exact through degree $383$. Moreover, $$\sum_{a=1}^{N/2-1}\frac{2}{4a^2-1}=1-\frac1{N-1}$$ by telescoping. Since every cosine is at most one, this identity and the case $l=0$ above prove $$\label{eq:positive-quadrature-weights}
 o_v\ge\frac1{N(N-1)}>0,\qquad \sum_{v=0}^{N-1}o_v=1.$$ The substitution $t=(2j-1+x)/12$ changes the average rule on $[-1,1]$ into the weights $o_v/6$ on the $j$th frequency interval. Each interval therefore has total weight $1/6$, and all six together have total weight one.

A tilde will denote this replacement of the density integrals. In particular, $\widetilde p$ and $\widetilde K_p$ are finite sums. The functions ultimately used in the Fourier pair remain $p$ and $K_p$ with their exact spectral integrals; the following lemma quantifies the difference.

<a id="stmt-lem-quadrature-error"></a>
<div id="lem:quadrature-error" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Quadrature error for scaled moments). *Let $p$ and $K_p$ be the exact direct and transformed functions for one basis input among the $171$ coordinates $(\mathcal F,e)$. For every integer $a\ge0$, real $0\le m\le168$, and real $|y|\le3/2$, the replacement <a href="#eq:quadrature-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadrature-rule">[eq:quadrature-rule]</a>, with all atoms kept exactly, changes each of $$\frac{y^a}{a!}p^{(a)}(m),\qquad
 \frac{y^a}{a!}K_p^{(a)}(m)$$ by less than $10^{-31}$ in absolute value.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-quadrature-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-quadrature-error</summary>

*Proof.* We first bound each analytic integrand on a disk twice as wide as its real integration interval. We then use polynomial exactness and positivity to turn one Taylor remainder bound into a quadrature error bound. The estimates are uniform in the derivative order.

For the $j$th density piece in <a href="#eq:folded-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:folded-density">[eq:folded-density]</a>, put $u=(2j-1)/12$ and consider $|t-u|\le1/6$. On this disk, $|t|\le13/12<1.1$. The pole $-ih$ of $z$ and $\lambda$ lies outside, and $$|\lambda(t)|\le\frac1{b(h-1/6)}<5,
 \qquad
 |z(t)|\le\frac{B}{h-1/6}+h=\frac{214}{35}<6.2.$$ Inversion sends the disk centered at $u+ih$ with radius $1/6$ to the disk centered at $(u-ih)/(u^2+h^2-1/36)$ with radius $(1/6)/(u^2+h^2-1/36)$. Thus <a id="eq:complex-kernel-disk"></a>$$\label{eq:complex-kernel-disk}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)\ge
 \frac{B(h-1/6)}{u^2+h^2-1/36}-h
 \ge-\frac{1402}{17505}>-.081.$$ The middle expression decreases as $u$ increases, so its minimum among the six disks occurs at $u=11/12$.

For positive indices, the moduli of the coefficients in <a href="#eq:periodic-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:periodic-coefficients">[eq:periodic-coefficients]</a> are $(2,2,2,1,2,1)$. Hence $$\sum_{l=1}^6|P_l|=10,\qquad
 \sum_{l=1}^6t_l|P_l|=\frac{16}{3}.$$ For a $c_n$ basis column, the density on the disk has modulus at most $$2\pi^2e^{\pi n/6}\sum_{l=j}^6|P_l|(t_l-u+1/6)
 \le\frac{37\pi^2}{3}e^{\pi M/6}<220e^{\pi M/6}.$$ The last sum is bounded by $16/3+(1/12)10=37/6$; the first disk gives the largest possible added term. For a $d_n$ column the smaller bound $20\pi e^{\pi M/6}$ suffices. These arguments include $n=0$. The constant column has only atoms, so it has no quadrature error.

The direct scaled kernel factor satisfies $$|e^{i\pi tm}|\frac{|i\pi ty|^a}{a!}
 \le e^{\pi M/6}e^{1.65\pi},$$ and the transformed factor satisfies $$|\lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)m}|\frac{|i\pi z(t)y|^a}{a!}
 \le5e^{.081\pi M}e^{9.3\pi}.$$ Here $x^a/a!\le e^x$ for $x\ge0$ removes all dependence on $a$. The resulting direct and transformed integrand bounds are respectively $$220e^{\pi M/3+1.65\pi},\qquad
 1100e^{\pi M/6+.081\pi M+9.3\pi}.$$ For completeness, the elementary estimates used to bound these two numbers need no matrix or interval computation. Polynomial division gives $$0<\int_0^1\frac{x^4(1-x)^4}{1+x^2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx=\frac{22}{7}-\pi.$$ The positive exponential series, whose tail after degree six is bounded by a geometric series of ratio $1/8$, gives $$e<\sum_{j=0}^6\frac1{j!}+\frac1{7!(1-1/8)}
   =\frac{95901}{35280}<\frac{2719}{1000}.$$ With $M=168$, the two exponents above are less than $182$ and $160$, respectively, after using $\pi<22/7$. The exact rational comparisons $$220(2719/1000)^{182}<10^{83},\qquad
 1100(2719/1000)^{160}<10^{83}$$ therefore bound both analytic integrands by $10^{83}$. This is the degree-six elementary route for the present allowance; it is separate from the scalar method developed in Appendix <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">9</a>.

The actual interval is $|t-u|\le1/12$, half the disk radius. Cauchy’s coefficient estimate bounds the Taylor tail after degree $383$ there by $$10^{83}\sum_{r=384}^{\infty}2^{-r}
 =2\cdot10^{83}2^{-384}.$$ The degree-$383$ polynomial is integrated exactly. Both integration and quadrature are positive functionals of total mass one when the six intervals are combined, by <a href="#eq:positive-quadrature-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-quadrature-weights">[eq:positive-quadrature-weights]</a>. Applying each to the tail thus bounds their difference by $$4\cdot10^{83}2^{-384}<10^{-31};$$ the final comparison is the integer inequality $4\cdot10^{114}<2^{384}$. Taking real parts cannot enlarge the error, and no atom was changed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Masses and transformed jets

The quadrature is now both specified and controlled. We next express it as matrix multiplication, so that every entry of the finite construction is determined by the formulas already given. The mass matrix $W$ has $6N=2304$ quadrature rows in lexicographic $(j,v)$ order, followed by the seven atom rows in increasing $j$ order; its $171$ columns use the coordinate order specified above. For $t=\tau_{jv}$ and $n\in\mathcal F\cup\{0\}$, set $$\begin{aligned}
 W_{(j,v),c_n}
 &=-\frac{2\pi^2o_v}{6}e^{-i\pi tn}
       \sum_{l=j}^6(t_l-t)P_le^{i\pi t_ln},\\
 W_{(j,v),d_n}
 &=\frac{2\pi i o_v}{6}e^{-i\pi tn}
       \sum_{l=j}^6P_le^{i\pi t_ln},&
 W_{(j,v),C}&=0.
\end{aligned}$$ At atom $j$, only the constant column is nonzero: $W_{\mathrm{atom}(j),C}=e_jP_j$, where $e_0=1$ and $e_j=2$ for $j>0$. These are exactly the folded masses of Lemma <a href="#lem:cardinal-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cardinal-measure">3</a>.

For the tuple $t$ of all $2311$ mass points, define the row vectors <a id="eq:quadrature-moments"></a>$$\label{eq:quadrature-moments}
\begin{aligned}
 F_a(m,y;t)&=e^{i\pi tm}\frac{(i\pi yt)^a}{a!},\\
 G_a(m,y;t)&=\lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)m}
                  \frac{(i\pi yz(t))^a}{a!}.
\end{aligned}$$ For a real coefficient column $A$, the real parts of $F_aWA$ and $G_aWA$ are the order-$a$ derivatives at $m$ of its direct and transformed finite sums, multiplied by $y^a/a!$. This follows by differentiating each finite exponential sum. It fixes, in particular, the absence of conjugation in these products.

Apply the jet projection of <a href="#eq:jet-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jet-projection">[eq:jet-projection]</a> to the transformed sums and write $$(\widetilde S_{\mathcal F\mathcal F},\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e})
 =-\mathcal J_{\mathcal F}\widetilde K,
 \qquad D=\widetilde S_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}.$$ Thus $D$ is a $168\times168$ matrix; it is distinct from the scalar node constants $D_n$. Explicitly, if $a_n=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(G_0(n,1)W)$ and $b_n=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(G_1(n,1)W)$, the $c_n$ and $d_n$ rows of the full projected matrix are $$-a_n/Q_n,\qquad (D_na_n-b_n)/Q_n,$$ respectively. The bounds in <a href="#eq:node-constant-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:node-constant-bounds">[eq:node-constant-bounds]</a> ensure that these denominators are positive.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Target data and finite reference columns

<a id="eq:extra-data"></a>
The ten data coordinates encode the target jets at the first five positive nodes. Define $$(n_1,\ldots,n_5)=(1,3,4,7,9),\qquad
 v_j=e^{-k(n_j-1)},\qquad
 q(k)=(v_1,\ldots,v_5,v_1/k,\ldots,v_5/k)^t.$$ The first five coordinates are $-T_k'(n_j)$ and the last five are $T_k(n_j)$. We will convert these values and slopes to cardinal coordinates with the jet projection $\mathcal J$. In the cardinal formula, $c_0,d_0$ determine the value and slope of the direct profile $p$ at the origin, while $C$ multiplies $P$ and supplies the constant term in the later rational tail comparison. These three coordinates leave all direct positive-node jets unchanged, but alter the transformed contribution. We fix them first; Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">4</a> will correct the positive-node coefficients to impose all contacts of the coupled pair. We choose the following values, whose suitability will be established by the norm and sign estimates below. In the order of list 1 followed by list 2, they are $$\label{eq:extra-data}
 E(k)=
 \begin{pmatrix}.567\\.250\\-.0074\\0\\-.209\\.0096\end{pmatrix}
 +\frac1k
 \begin{pmatrix}-.976\\.408\\.0115\\1\\.938\\-.017\end{pmatrix}.$$ Let $E_1,E_2$ be $3\times10$ matrices. Their first columns are the first and last three entries, respectively, of the first vector in <a href="#eq:extra-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extra-data">[eq:extra-data]</a>; their sixth columns use the corresponding entries of the second vector, and all other columns vanish. Since $v_1=1$, the stack of $E_1q(k)$ and $E_2q(k)$ is exactly $E(k)$.

Define a $168\times10$ matrix $Y$ by the following nonzero entries, for $1\le j\le5$: $$Y_{d_{n_j},j}=-\frac1{Q_{n_j}},\qquad
 Y_{c_{n_j},j+5}=\frac1{Q_{n_j}},\qquad
 Y_{d_{n_j},j+5}=-\frac{D_{n_j}}{Q_{n_j}}.$$ Then $Yq(k)$ is the jet projection of $T_k$ at these five nodes and is zero at the other nodes of $\mathcal F$. The remaining target jets are small; Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">4</a> will include them in the correction.

<a id="eq:finite-columns"></a>
To see which equations the finite columns must approximate, write $A_1,A_2$ for their unknown positive-node parts, each a $168\times10$ matrix. The coupled jet identities from Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a>, with integrals replaced by quadrature and target data replaced by $Y$, give $$A_1-DA_2=Y+\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e}E_2,
 \qquad
 A_2-DA_1=\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e}E_1.$$ Each right side contains the other list’s extras because the transformed function in $H_i$ comes from that other list. Denote these right sides by $g_1,g_2$. Adding and subtracting the equations gives $$(I-D)(A_1+A_2)=g_1+g_2,
 \qquad
 (I+D)(A_1-A_2)=g_1-g_2.$$ We use finite geometric sums in place of the two inverses and define $$\label{eq:finite-columns}
\begin{aligned}
 g_1&=Y+\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e}E_2,&
 g_2&=\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e}E_1,\\
 V_+&=\sum_{j=0}^{511}D^j,&
 V_-&=\sum_{j=0}^{511}(-D)^j,\\
 X_{1\mathcal F}
 &=\tfrac12\bigl(V_+(g_1+g_2)+V_-(g_1-g_2)\bigr),\\
 X_{2\mathcal F}
 &=\tfrac12\bigl(V_+(g_1+g_2)-V_-(g_1-g_2)\bigr),&
 X_i&=\begin{pmatrix}X_{i\mathcal F}\\E_i\end{pmatrix}\quad(i=1,2).
\end{aligned}$$ The identities $(I-D)V_+=(I+D)V_-=I-D^{512}$ show that the error in these equations is controlled by $D^{512}$. Its bound, and the passage back to the exact integrals, are proved in Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">4</a>. Each $X_i$ has $171$ rows and ten columns; the reference list for a given $k$ will be $X_iq(k)$. A column is extended by zero beyond $M$ when regarded as a full list. For any such matrix $X$, the row functions $p_X$ and $K_X$ have $j$th components $p_{(X)_j}$ and $K_{p_{(X)_j}}$; the same convention applies to finite sums and derivatives. Although the reference lists have finite support, their profiles $p_{X_iq(k)}$ and $K_{p_{X_iq(k)}}$ use the exact spectral integrals. The tilde profiles replace those integrals by the quadrature rule when constructing finite moment rows.

The remaining finite bounds have three roles. Bounds on powers of $D$ and the sums $V_+,V_-$ will control the finite inverse in Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">4</a>, while column norms will control the size of the reference lists and their interpolation residual. Half-gap and tail inequalities formed from the reference columns will supply quantitative margins in the proof of $T_k-H_1\ge0$ and $H_2\ge0$. Section <a href="#sec:signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:signs">5</a> will compare these inequalities with the exact pair, accounting for the interpolation correction and the approximation errors. We next construct the rows for those sign inequalities.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Containing parameter boxes

<a id="eq:box-lower"></a>
The data vector $q(k)$ lies on a one-dimensional curve. To obtain lower bounds uniform in $k$, we place each part of that curve in a rectangular box. The endpoints are $$2.36,\quad2.65,\quad3.2,\quad4,\quad6,\quad\infty.$$ For a real row $l=(a_1,\ldots,a_5,b_1,\ldots,b_5)$ and consecutive endpoints $K,J$, define $$\label{eq:box-lower}
 [l]_{K,J}=\min_{x\in\{1/K,1/J\}}\left(
 a_1+xb_1+\sum_{j=2}^5
 \min_{H\in\{K,J\}}\{(a_j+xb_j)e^{-H(n_j-1)}\}\right).$$ At $J=\infty$, the conventions are $1/J=0$ and $e^{-J(n_j-1)}=0$ for $j>1$.

<a id="stmt-lem-parameter-box"></a>
<div id="lem:parameter-box" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Containing parameter boxes). *Let $K,J$ be consecutive endpoints in the displayed list. For every real row $l$ as above and every finite $k$ with $K\le k\le J$, where the upper inequality is omitted if $J=\infty$, one has $lq(k)\ge[l]_{K,J}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-parameter-box"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-parameter-box</summary>

*Proof.* Write $x=1/k$ and $w_j=e^{-k(n_j-1)}$ for $j=2,\ldots,5$. The physical vector lies in the box $$x\in[1/J,1/K],\qquad
 w_j\in[e^{-J(n_j-1)},e^{-K(n_j-1)}].$$ On this larger box the expression $a_1+xb_1+\sum_{j=2}^5(a_j+xb_j)w_j$ is affine in each variable while the others are fixed. Its minimum therefore occurs at a vertex. First minimizing each $w_j$ and then the single shared $x$ gives exactly <a href="#eq:box-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:box-lower">[eq:box-lower]</a>. This is a containing-box bound, not sampling of the curve $q(k)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Polynomial rows on the half-gaps

We now construct the rows to which the containing-box bound will be applied. They are Bernstein coefficients of polynomials that will control the signs between nodes. The required inequalities are $T_k-H_1\ge0$ and $H_2\ge0$, so set $\sigma_1=-1$ and $\sigma_2=1$. At each center $m\in\mathcal N_0\cap[0,88]$, take the half-gap toward its successor and, when $m>0$, the half-gap toward its predecessor in $\mathcal N_0$. If $m'$ is that neighbor, put $y=(m'-m)/2$. The half-gap consists of $s=m+yx$, $0\le x\le1$, with signed $y$.

<a id="eq:bernstein-rows"></a>
At a positive center the desired value and derivative contacts would make $T_k-H_1$ and $H_2$ vanish to second order. Before making an approximation, we therefore subtract these two Taylor terms from each component. For an entire function $u$, define $$\mathcal D_{m,2}u(s)
 =\frac{u(s)-u(m)-(s-m)u'(m)}{(s-m)^2},
 \qquad \mathcal D_{m,2}u(m)=\frac{u''(m)}2.$$ If $u(m)=u'(m)=0$, then $$u(s)=(s-m)^2\mathcal D_{m,2}u(s).$$ Once exact interpolation is imposed, this identity applies to both $u=T_k-H_1$ and $u=H_2$ at every positive center. It reduces their sign inequalities to lower bounds for quotients that remain regular at the nodes. At the origin we use $\mathcal D_{0,0}u=u$. Put $\nu=0$ there and $\nu=2$ at every positive center. Taylor expansion gives $$\mathcal D_{m,\nu}u(m+yx)
 =\sum_{r=0}^\infty\frac{y^r}{(r+\nu)!}u^{(r+\nu)}(m)x^r.$$ This subtraction is defined even for reference functions that do not interpolate exactly. For the reference pair formed from the columns $X_i$, we keep the first 41 coefficients and replace each spectral moment by its quadrature sum. The resulting ten-component rows are $$\label{eq:bernstein-rows}
\begin{aligned}
 l_{i,r}&=\sigma_i\frac{y^r}{(r+\nu)!}
 \left(\widetilde p_{X_i}^{(r+\nu)}(m)
      +\widetilde K_{X_{3-i}}^{(r+\nu)}(m)\right),&&0\le r\le40,\\
 L_{i,d}&=\sum_{r=0}^d\omega_{dr}l_{i,r},&
 \omega_{dr}&=\frac{\binom dr}{\binom{40}r},\qquad0\le d\le40.
\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, the first row is $$l_{i,r}=\sigma_i y^{-\nu}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(
 F_{r+\nu}(m,y)WX_i+G_{r+\nu}(m,y)WX_{3-i}\bigr).$$ There is no singular case here: every selected half-gap has $y\ne0$.

The rows $L_{i,d}$ are exactly the degree-$40$ Bernstein coefficients of $\sum_{r=0}^{40}l_{i,r}x^r$. Indeed, the binomial theorem gives $$x^r=\sum_{d=r}^{40}\frac{\binom dr}{\binom{40}r}
       \binom{40}d x^d(1-x)^{40-d}.$$ On $[0,1]$ the complete family $\binom{40}d x^d(1-x)^{40-d}$, $0\le d\le40$, is nonnegative and sums to one. Thus a polynomial value lies between its smallest and largest Bernstein coefficients, the standard coefficient-range enclosure discussed by Titi and Garloff (Titi and Garloff 2019, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[TitiGarloff2019](#ref-TitiGarloff2019)]. The identity just proved specifies our rows; the local remainder and parameter estimates are separate.

<a id="eq:target-support"></a>
The first inequality also contains the target quotient $\mathcal D_{m,\nu}T_k$. To bound it below on the same half-gap and parameter box, define $$\label{eq:target-support}
 (a_r^*)_{r=0}^{40}=
 \begin{cases}
 \bigl((e^K/K)(-Ky)^r/r!\bigr)_{r=0}^{40},&m=0,\\
 \bigl(K(-Ky)^r/(r+2)!\bigr)_{r=0}^{40},&m=1,\\
 \bigl((J/2)e^{-13J/6},0,\ldots,0\bigr),&m=3,\ J<\infty,\\
 (0,\ldots,0),&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:exponential-support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exponential-support">11</a> proves the precise lower bound $$\mathcal D_{m,\nu}T_k(m+yx)
 \ge\sum_{r=0}^{40}a_r^*x^r-.000001\qquad(0\le x\le1).$$ Thus the target polynomial supports the same quotient as the reference polynomial; its truncation error is retained separately.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The complete finite certificate

For large $s$, we will use the low-node terms of the cardinal formula rather than its Taylor expansion. Put $$s_0=89.5,\qquad \mathcal N_{\le88}=\mathcal N_0\cap[0,88].$$ Write $u_{i,n}$ and $d^*_{i,n}$ for the $c_n$ and $d_n$ rows of $\sigma_iX_i$, and $C_i^*$ for its constant row. For the reference list $X_iq(k)$, the signed rational part from these rows is $$\sigma_iR_{i,0}^{\mathrm{ref}}(s)
 =C_i^*q(k)+\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_{\le88}}
 \left(\frac{u_{i,n}q(k)}{(s-n)^2}
       +\frac{d^*_{i,n}q(k)}{s-n}\right).$$ The identity $$\frac1{s-n}=\frac1s+\frac{n}{s(s-n)}$$ groups the simple-pole rows into a coefficient of $1/s$ and a remaining sum. For a fixed parameter box, put $\eta_{K,J}(l)=\min(0,[l]_{K,J})$. This gives a nonpositive lower bound for the numerator $lq(k)$. On $s\ge s_0$, the reciprocal factors are positive and decrease with $s$, so their products with this lower bound are smallest at $s_0$. Keeping the full lower bound for the constant row gives the tail expression in the proposition below. In Section <a href="#sec:signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:signs">5</a>, its positive margin will absorb the correction to the exact lists and the omitted high-node and transformed terms.

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-certificate"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-certificate" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:bernstein-certificate"></a>
<a id="eq:tail-certificate"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Finite certificate). *The exact arrays defined in this section satisfy <a id="eq:finite-norms"></a>$$\label{eq:finite-norms}
\begin{gathered}
 \left\lVert D^{64}\right\rVert<.004,\qquad \left\lVert V_+\right\rVert+\left\lVert V_-\right\rVert<36,\\
 \sum_{i=1}^2\left\lVert(X_i)_j\right\rVert_1<
 \begin{cases}2.3,&1\le j\le5,\\5.6,&6\le j\le10.\end{cases}
\end{gathered}$$ For every specified half-gap, $i=1,2$, consecutive parameter endpoints $K,J$, and $0\le d\le40$, $$\label{eq:bernstein-certificate}
 [L_{i,d}]_{K,J}
 +\mathbf1_{i=1}\sum_{r=0}^d\omega_{dr}a_r^*>.001.$$ For every box and $i=1,2$, $$\label{eq:tail-certificate}
\begin{split}
 [C_i^*]_{K,J}
 &+\frac{\eta_{K,J}\bigl(\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_{\le88}}d^*_{i,n}\bigr)}{s_0}\\
 &+\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_{\le88}}\left(
 \frac{\eta_{K,J}(u_{i,n})}{(s_0-n)^2}
 +\frac{n\,\eta_{K,J}(d^*_{i,n})}{s_0(s_0-n)}\right)>.0021.
\end{split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-finite-certificate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-finite-certificate</summary>

*Finite verification.* We specify the complete stronger exact-array comparisons in Tables <a href="#table:power-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="table:power-bounds">1</a>–<a href="#table:sign-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="table:sign-bounds">3</a> and the route by which each is checked. Their looser consequences are the proposition’s outputs, whose analytic use is separate. No precomputed coefficient list or approximate auxiliary function is an input: all arrays are reconstructed from <a href="#eq:quadrature-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadrature-rule">[eq:quadrature-rule]</a>– <a href="#eq:finite-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-columns">[eq:finite-columns]</a>.

The power sums use nine doubling steps. Start with $U=D$ and $V_+=V_-=I$. At step $r=0,\ldots,8$, replace $V_+$ by $V_+(I+U)$ and $V_-$ by $V_-(I+\epsilon_rU)$, where $\epsilon_0=-1$ and $\epsilon_r=1$ for $r>0$; square $U$ before the next step. Before step $r$, $U=D^{2^r}$, and $$\prod_{r=0}^8(I+D^{2^r})=\sum_{j=0}^{511}D^j,
 \qquad
 (I-D)\prod_{r=1}^8(I+D^{2^r})=\sum_{j=0}^{511}(-D)^j.$$ Thus the final sums are exactly those in <a href="#eq:finite-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-columns">[eq:finite-columns]</a>. Table <a href="#table:power-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="table:power-bounds">1</a> gives strict upper bounds for $U$ before each update and for both sums after it. Row 6 therefore bounds $D^{64}$.

<div id="table:power-bounds">

| Step $r$ | $\left\lVert U\right\rVert$ | $\left\lVert V_+\right\rVert$ | $\left\lVert V_-\right\rVert$ |
|---------:|----------------------------:|------------------------------:|------------------------------:|
|        0 |                        1.74 |                          2.74 |                           2.1 |
|        1 |                        2.09 |                          6.94 |                           2.2 |
|        2 |                        2.27 |                         15.32 |                          2.13 |
|        3 |                        1.75 |                          25.3 |                          1.97 |
|        4 |                         .75 |                          31.1 |                          1.88 |
|        5 |                         .12 |                         32.09 |                          1.85 |
|        6 |                       .0031 |                         32.12 |                          1.85 |
|        7 |                     .000002 |                         32.12 |                          1.85 |
|        8 |               .000000000001 |                         32.12 |                          1.85 |

Strict upper bounds in the nine-step power computation.

</div>

<div id="table:column-bounds">

| Quantity                                       | Columns $1$–$5$ | Columns $6$–$10$ |
|:-----------------------------------------------|----------------:|-----------------:|
| $\max_j\left\lVert(X_1)_j\right\rVert_1$       |            1.29 |             2.65 |
| $\max_j\left\lVert(X_2)_j\right\rVert_1$       |            1.01 |             3.03 |
| $\max_j\sum_i\left\lVert(X_i)_j\right\rVert_1$ |            2.20 |             5.40 |

Strict upper bounds for column norms. In the last row the two norms are added for the same column before taking the maximum.

</div>

<div id="table:sign-bounds">

|         | Half-gap certificate |       |         |               |       |
|--------:|---------------------:|------:|--------:|--------------:|------:|
| 2-5 $K$ |                $m=0$ | $m=1$ | $m=3,4$ | $7\le m\le88$ |  Tail |
|    2.36 |                  .33 |  .019 |   .0033 |        .00116 | .0022 |
|    2.65 |                  .34 |  .017 |   .0037 |         .0018 | .0029 |
|     3.2 |                  .35 |  .033 |   .0061 |         .0026 | .0036 |
|       4 |                  .36 |  .036 |   .0085 |         .0031 | .0043 |
|       6 |                  .38 |  .011 |   .0105 |         .0037 | .0052 |

Strict lower bounds for the left sides of <a href="#eq:bernstein-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bernstein-certificate">[eq:bernstein-certificate]</a> and <a href="#eq:tail-certificate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-certificate">[eq:tail-certificate]</a>. Each half-gap entry covers both functions, every indicated center and signed half-gap, and all Bernstein indices. The right endpoint $J$ follows $K$ in the endpoint list.

</div>

There are $46$ centers: the seven complete residue blocks in $[0,83]$ contribute $42$, and $84,85,87,88$ contribute four more. Every center has a right half-gap and every center except zero has a left one, giving $91$. Consequently the sign table consists of $91\cdot2\cdot5\cdot41=37310$ separate real lower comparisons, and the tail column consists of ten. The power and column tests likewise compare every indicated norm, including each same-column paired norm.

The selected finite route encloses the exact mathematical quadrature arrays directly by Arb ball arithmetic, whose inclusion principle is described by Johansson (Johansson 2017) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Johansson2017](#ref-Johansson2017)]. The local source implements these formulas at $256$-bit precision. For an upper comparison the required test is that the whole ball lies below its table cutoff; for a lower comparison it is that the whole ball lies above it. It performs these tests for all indices just counted. All comparisons in Tables <a href="#table:power-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="table:power-bounds">1</a>–<a href="#table:sign-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="table:sign-bounds">3</a> pass these strict inclusion tests. The containing-box lemma, Bernstein range property, and analytic remainders provide continuum coverage in $s$ and $k$.

Finally, the stronger cutoffs imply the displayed proposition bounds: $.0031<.004$, $32.12+1.85<36$, $2.20<2.3$, $5.40<5.6$, $.00116>.001$, and $.0022>.0021$. Appendix <a href="#app:floor-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:floor-certificate">11</a> gives a second, fully specified floor-rounded rational procedure and proves that a successful comparison with these same stronger cutoffs implies the looser proposition bounds after its roundoff allowances. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:interpolation"></a>
### Exact interpolation in the summable coefficient space

The finite columns of Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a> approximate the interpolation equations, but the auxiliary functions must use the genuine spectral integrals. We correct the finite lists in an unweighted $\ell^1$ space, holding their extra coordinates fixed. The useful small quantity is not the norm of the full transformed-jet operator: it is the norm of its outputs at distant nodes. This allows a finite-block inverse followed by a small tail correction.

The elimination method adapts the finite-block and tail argument of OpenAI (OpenAI 2026a, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[SharpPlanar2026](#ref-SharpPlanar2026)]. The operator, target data, and quantitative estimates below are those of the present Gaussian problem.

Let $$\mathcal E=\ell^1(\mathcal N;\mathbb R^2),\qquad
 \mathcal T=\mathcal N\setminus\mathcal F,$$ where $\mathcal F=\mathcal N\cap[1,168]$ is the finite node set from Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a>. The two coordinates at each node are its $c$- and $d$-coefficients. On a pair of lists we use the sum of their norms. A subscript $\mathcal F$ or $\mathcal T$ restricts the corresponding input or output; the paired finite block has 336 coordinates. Every operator norm is induced by these unweighted norms.

<a id="stmt-prop-exact-interpolation"></a>
<div id="prop:exact-interpolation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:exact-jets"></a>
<a id="eq:exact-list-bounds"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Exact interpolation). *For every real $k\ge2.36$, there is a unique pair of positive-node lists in $\mathcal E$, with extra coordinates $e_i=E_iq(k)$, whose full lists $\ell_i$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 H_1(n)&=T_k(n),& H_1'(n)&=T_k'(n),\\
 H_2(n)&=0,& H_2'(n)&=0
 \end{aligned}
 \qquad(n\in\mathcal N),
 \label{eq:exact-jets}$$ where $H_1=p_{\ell_1}+K_{p_{\ell_2}}$ and $H_2=p_{\ell_2}+K_{p_{\ell_1}}$. Let $\ell_i^{\mathrm{ref}}=X_iq(k)$, extended by zero on $\mathcal T$. Then $$\sum_{i=1}^2\left\lVert\ell_i^{\mathrm{ref}}\right\rVert_1<4.8,\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^2\left\lVert\ell_i-\ell_i^{\mathrm{ref}}\right\rVert_1<2\cdot10^{-8},\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^2\left\lVert\ell_i\right\rVert_1<5.
 \label{eq:exact-list-bounds}$$ The functions $H_1,H_2$ are entire and real on the real axis. In particular, $H_1-T_k$ and $H_2$ have zeros of order at least two at every positive node, including the nodes that are not lattice radii. Their Gaussian-damped functions form the real radial Schwartz Fourier pair of Lemma <a href="#lem:fourier-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fourier-pair">5</a>.*

</div>

The bounded inverse below gives uniqueness for summable coefficient lists with fixed extras and jets prescribed on the larger periodic node set $\mathcal N$. This differs from reconstruction using only the actual triangular shells: Talebizadeh Sardari (Talebizadeh Sardari 2021, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Sardari2021](#ref-Sardari2021)] constructs infinitely many linearly independent radial Schwartz functions whose values and radial derivatives, and those of their Fourier transforms, vanish on all those shells.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-exact-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-exact-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* We first solve the exact operator equation on the full space. We then bound the size and residual of the finite reference lists and use the same block equations to estimate their correction.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### The coupled jet equation.

<a id="eq:infinite-interpolation-system"></a>
<a id="eq:target-jet-bound"></a>
Write $a_i\in\mathcal E$ for the positive-node part of $\ell_i$. By <a href="#eq:cardinal-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cardinal-jets">[eq:cardinal-jets]</a> and <a href="#eq:jet-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jet-operator">[eq:jet-operator]</a>, the desired equations are $$\mathcal L\binom{a_1}{a_2}
 :=\begin{pmatrix}I&-S\\-S&I\end{pmatrix}\binom{a_1}{a_2}
 =\binom{\mathcal JT_k+S_e e_2}{S_e e_1}.
 \label{eq:infinite-interpolation-system}$$ Here $S$ acts on positive-node inputs, while $S_e$ acts on the three extra coordinates. Both are bounded by Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-bounds">6</a>. The target belongs to $\mathcal E$ as well. Indeed, $T_k'(n)=-e^{-k(n-1)}$, and <a href="#eq:node-constant-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:node-constant-bounds">[eq:node-constant-bounds]</a> gives $$\left|\frac{T_k(n)}{Q_n}\right|
 +\left|\frac{T_k'(n)-D_nT_k(n)}{Q_n}\right|
 \le\frac{1+(1+2.12)/k}{1.75}\,e^{-k(n-1)}.
 \label{eq:target-jet-bound}$$ The right side is summable, uniformly after replacing $k$ by 2.36. We will use the exact constant $$c_*:=\frac{1+(1+2.12)/2.36}{1.75}=\frac{548}{413}<\frac43.$$

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### The finite block.

Let $D=\widetilde S_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}$ and form the paired quadrature block $$\mathcal L_D=\begin{pmatrix}I&-D\\-D&I\end{pmatrix}.$$ With $\eta=.004^8$, submultiplicativity and Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-certificate">9</a> give $\left\lVert D^{512}\right\rVert=\left\lVert(D^{64})^8\right\rVert<\eta$. The finite geometric identities for the matrices $V_+,V_-$ in <a href="#eq:finite-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-columns">[eq:finite-columns]</a> yield $$R_+=(I-D)^{-1}=V_+(I-D^{512})^{-1},\qquad
 R_-=(I+D)^{-1}=V_-(I-D^{512})^{-1}.$$ The remaining inverse is its norm-convergent Neumann series. Diagonalizing the two-list block into its sum and difference gives $$\mathcal L_D^{-1}=\frac12
 \begin{pmatrix}
 R_++R_-&R_+-R_-\\
 R_+-R_-&R_++R_-
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ Bounding the two output blocks in each column, and using the finite sum-norm bound, gives <a id="eq:finite-quadrature-inverse"></a>$$\left\lVert\mathcal L_D^{-1}\right\rVert
 \le\left\lVert R_+\right\rVert+\left\lVert R_-\right\rVert
 \le\frac{\left\lVert V_+\right\rVert+\left\lVert V_-\right\rVert}{1-\eta}
 <\frac{36}{1-\eta}<37.
 \label{eq:finite-quadrature-inverse}$$

<a id="eq:finite-exact-inverse"></a>
We now replace this quadrature block by the exact one. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:quadrature-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadrature-error">7</a> to orders zero and one with $y=1$. For each basis input, the errors in the two transformed moments are less than $10^{-31}$. In the jet projection their multipliers are bounded by $1/Q_n$ and $(1+|D_n|)/Q_n$, both less than 2. In particular, every entry of $S_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}-D$, and every entry of $S_{\mathcal F e}-\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e}$, has absolute value less than $10^{-29}$. There are 168 output coordinates. The paired block is off-diagonal, so its column norm has no extra factor of two. Hence <a id="eq:finite-operator-perturbation"></a>$$\left\lVert\mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}-\mathcal L_D\right\rVert
 <2\cdot10^{-27}.
 \label{eq:finite-operator-perturbation}$$ Factoring $\mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}$ through $\mathcal L_D$ and using another Neumann series gives $$\left\lVert\mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}^{-1}\right\rVert
 <\frac{37}{1-37\cdot2\cdot10^{-27}}<40.
 \label{eq:finite-exact-inverse}$$ This bound is for the exact finite block, not for the full inverse.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### Solving after elimination of the finite block.

<a id="eq:tail-operator-blocks"></a>
The scalar bound <a href="#eq:scalar-error-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-error-budgets">[eq:scalar-error-budgets]</a> gives $$U_{168}(1,0)<1.1\cdot10^{-14}=:\delta.$$ Because <a href="#eq:tail-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-bound">[eq:tail-bound]</a> is uniform in the input node locations, it bounds outputs on $\mathcal T$ from either finite or infinite positive-node inputs. Again the two off-diagonal blocks introduce no extra factor in the combined $\ell^1$ norm. We obtain $$\left\lVert\mathcal L_{\mathcal T\mathcal F}\right\rVert<\delta,\qquad
 \left\lVert\mathcal L_{\mathcal T\mathcal T}-I\right\rVert<\delta,\qquad
 \left\lVert\mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal T}\right\rVert<4000.
 \label{eq:tail-operator-blocks}$$ The last bound need not be small. We first solve the finite equation for its finite unknowns in terms of the tail unknowns. Substituting that solution into the tail equation leaves the Schur complement $$\mathcal Q=\mathcal L_{\mathcal T\mathcal T}
 -\mathcal L_{\mathcal T\mathcal F}
  \mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}^{-1}
  \mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal T}.$$ It satisfies $$\left\lVert\mathcal Q-I\right\rVert
 <\delta(1+40\cdot4000)=1.760011\cdot10^{-9}<1,$$ so its Neumann series converges. More explicitly, for any right side $\psi=(\psi_{\mathcal F},\psi_{\mathcal T})$, the solution is reconstructed by $$\begin{aligned}
 a_{\mathcal T}
 &=\mathcal Q^{-1}\bigl(\psi_{\mathcal T}
   -\mathcal L_{\mathcal T\mathcal F}
    \mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}^{-1}\psi_{\mathcal F}\bigr),\\
 a_{\mathcal F}
 &=\mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}^{-1}
   \bigl(\psi_{\mathcal F}-\mathcal L_{\mathcal F\mathcal T}a_{\mathcal T}\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ Every operator in these formulas is bounded. Thus $\mathcal L$ is a boundedly invertible operator on the full pair space $\mathcal E^2$, not merely on a truncation. Applied to <a href="#eq:infinite-interpolation-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:infinite-interpolation-system">[eq:infinite-interpolation-system]</a>, this proves existence and uniqueness for the specified extras. We henceforth write $a=(a_1,a_2)$ for this solution.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Size of the reference lists.

<a id="eq:reference-norm"></a>
<a id="eq:extra-constant-bound"></a>
The same-column bounds in Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-certificate">9</a>, applied to the nonnegative entries of $q(k)$, give $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_i\left\lVert\ell_i^{\mathrm{ref}}\right\rVert_1
 &\le\left(2.3+\frac{5.6}{k}\right)
       \left(1+e^{-2k}+e^{-3k}+e^{-6k}+e^{-8k}\right)\notag\\
 &\le\left(2.3+\frac{5.6}{2.36}\right)
       \left(1+e^{-4.72}+e^{-7.08}+e^{-14.16}+e^{-18.88}\right)
 <4.8.
 \label{eq:reference-norm}
\end{aligned}$$ The last comparison is the corresponding scalar bound in <a href="#eq:scalar-error-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-error-budgets">[eq:scalar-error-budgets]</a>. The norm includes all extras, so $\left\lVert e_1\right\rVert_1+\left\lVert e_2\right\rVert_1<4.8$. The constant coordinates in <a href="#eq:extra-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extra-data">[eq:extra-data]</a> satisfy the sharper bound $$|C_1|+|C_2|
 =\left|-.0074+\frac{.0115}{k}\right|
  +\left|.0096-\frac{.017}{k}\right|<.02.
 \label{eq:extra-constant-bound}$$ Indeed, for $k\ge2.36$, the first expression inside absolute values is negative and the second is positive; their absolute values are less than $.0074$ and $.0096$, respectively. The exact lists have the same constants because their extras are fixed.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### The three finite residual terms.

Let $a^{\mathrm{ref}}$ be the paired positive-node part of the reference lists, and let $r$ be the right side of <a href="#eq:infinite-interpolation-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:infinite-interpolation-system">[eq:infinite-interpolation-system]</a> minus $\mathcal L a^{\mathrm{ref}}$. Put $\gamma_i=g_iq(k)$, with $g_i$ as in <a href="#eq:finite-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-columns">[eq:finite-columns]</a>, and define $$\Delta S_{\mathcal F}
 =\bigl(S_{\mathcal F\mathcal F}-D,\,
        S_{\mathcal F e}-\widetilde S_{\mathcal F e}\bigr).$$ This map acts on the finite positive-node coordinates followed by the three extras; the reference lists are supported on these coordinates. The finite geometric identities $(I-D)V_+=(I+D)V_-=I-D^{512}$ give the exact decomposition <a id="eq:finite-residual-decomposition"></a>$$\label{eq:finite-residual-decomposition}
 r_{\mathcal F}
 =\binom{\mathcal J_{\mathcal F}T_k-Yq(k)}{0}
  +\binom{\Delta S_{\mathcal F}\ell_2^{\mathrm{ref}}}
         {\Delta S_{\mathcal F}\ell_1^{\mathrm{ref}}}
  +\binom{D^{512}\gamma_1}{D^{512}\gamma_2}.$$ The three terms are the omitted target data, the quadrature error on the full reference lists, and the finite geometric-sum residual.

The vector $Yq(k)$ reproduces the target jets at $1,3,4,7,9$ and vanishes at the other finite nodes. Summing <a href="#eq:target-jet-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-jet-bound">[eq:target-jet-bound]</a> over all integers from 12, a superset of the omitted nodes, and applying the scalar bound in <a href="#eq:scalar-error-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-error-budgets">[eq:scalar-error-budgets]</a>, gives <a id="eq:omitted-target-jets"></a>$$\frac{1+(1+2.12)/2.36}{1.75}
 \sum_{n=12}^{\infty}e^{-2.36(n-1)}<8\cdot10^{-12}.
 \label{eq:omitted-target-jets}$$ For the quadrature replacement, each basis column has finite output error less than $168\cdot10^{-29}$. Multiplication by the combined reference norm in <a href="#eq:reference-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-norm">[eq:reference-norm]</a> therefore costs less than $10^{-24}$, including the extra inputs.

<a id="eq:finite-reference-residual"></a>
For the third term, we can bound $\gamma_i$ without a further matrix estimate. The elementary bound $e^{-2.36}<2/21$, proved in the local calculation preceding <a href="#eq:target-tail-168" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-tail-168">[eq:target-tail-168]</a>, and <a href="#eq:target-jet-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-jet-bound">[eq:target-jet-bound]</a> give the first five target jets total norm at most $$c_*\left(1+(2/21)^2+(2/21)^3+(2/21)^6+(2/21)^8\right)<1.35.$$ The exact operator on the extra inputs has norm less than 4000, and its finite quadrature replacement differs by less than $2\cdot10^{-27}$. Since the extras have combined norm less than 4.8, $$\left\lVert\gamma_1\right\rVert_1+\left\lVert\gamma_2\right\rVert_1
 <1.35+(4000+2\cdot10^{-27})4.8<20000.$$ The geometric-sum residual is consequently less than $20000\eta=1.31072\cdot10^{-15}<1.4\cdot10^{-15}$. Adding the three finite errors yields $$\left\lVert r_{\mathcal F}\right\rVert_1<10^{-11}.
 \label{eq:finite-reference-residual}$$

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### The tail residual.

<a id="eq:tail-reference-residual"></a>
The positive-node reference lists vanish on $\mathcal T$. Their transformed outputs there, including the extras, are bounded by $U_{168}(5,.02)$, using <a href="#eq:reference-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-norm">[eq:reference-norm]</a> and <a href="#eq:extra-constant-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extra-constant-bound">[eq:extra-constant-bound]</a>. For the target part, $\mathcal T$ is contained in the integers above 168, so <a href="#eq:target-jet-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-jet-bound">[eq:target-jet-bound]</a> gives $$\sum_{n\in\mathcal T}
 \left(\left|\frac{T_k(n)}{Q_n}\right|
 +\left|\frac{T_k'(n)-D_nT_k(n)}{Q_n}\right|\right)
 \le c_*\frac{e^{-2.36\cdot168}}{1-e^{-2.36}}
 <10^{-170}.$$ The last inequality is the local target-tail calculation <a href="#eq:target-tail-168" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-tail-168">[eq:target-tail-168]</a>. The scalar bound <a href="#eq:scalar-error-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-error-budgets">[eq:scalar-error-budgets]</a> retains the separate strict margin $U_{168}(5,.02)<8.3\cdot10^{-14}-10^{-170}$. Therefore $$\left\lVert r_{\mathcal T}\right\rVert_1
 \le U_{168}(5,.02)+10^{-170}<8.3\cdot10^{-14}.
 \label{eq:tail-reference-residual}$$

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Correction size and exact jets.

<a id="eq:correction-system"></a>
Put $u_{\mathcal F}=\left\lVert(a-a^{\mathrm{ref}})_{\mathcal F}\right\rVert_1$ and $u_{\mathcal T}=\left\lVert(a-a^{\mathrm{ref}})_{\mathcal T}\right\rVert_1$. Take the finite and tail blocks of $\mathcal L(a-a^{\mathrm{ref}})=r$. Using <a href="#eq:finite-exact-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-exact-inverse">[eq:finite-exact-inverse]</a>, <a href="#eq:tail-operator-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-operator-blocks">[eq:tail-operator-blocks]</a>, <a href="#eq:finite-reference-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-reference-residual">[eq:finite-reference-residual]</a>, and <a href="#eq:tail-reference-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-reference-residual">[eq:tail-reference-residual]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 u_{\mathcal F}&\le40\bigl(10^{-11}+4000u_{\mathcal T}\bigr),\\
 u_{\mathcal T}&\le8.3\cdot10^{-14}
       +1.1\cdot10^{-14}(u_{\mathcal F}+u_{\mathcal T}).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:correction-system}$$ Substituting the first inequality into the second gives $$u_{\mathcal T}
 \le\frac{8.3\cdot10^{-14}+4.4\cdot10^{-24}}
          {1-1.760011\cdot10^{-9}}
 <8.31\cdot10^{-14},\qquad
 u_{\mathcal F}<1.37\cdot10^{-8}.$$ Their sum is less than $2\cdot10^{-8}$. The extras did not change, so this is the full-list correction in <a href="#eq:exact-list-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-list-bounds">[eq:exact-list-bounds]</a>. Adding <a href="#eq:reference-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-norm">[eq:reference-norm]</a> gives the combined exact norm less than 5.

Finally, <a href="#eq:infinite-interpolation-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:infinite-interpolation-system">[eq:infinite-interpolation-system]</a> is exactly $\mathcal JH_1=\mathcal JT_k$ and $\mathcal JH_2=0$. The definition of $\mathcal J$ recovers both values and first derivatives at every node of $\mathcal N$, proving <a href="#eq:exact-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-jets">[eq:exact-jets]</a>. The functions $p_{\ell_i}$ and $K_{p_{\ell_i}}$ are entire and real on the real axis by Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a>; $T_k$ is entire as well. The jet identities are therefore genuine double zeros. Lemma <a href="#lem:fourier-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fourier-pair">5</a> supplies the Schwartz Fourier pair. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:signs"></a>
### Signs on every radius and parameter

<a id="eq:exact-slack-jets"></a>
Fix a real $k\ge2.36$, and take the exact lists $\ell_1,\ell_2$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:exact-interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exact-interpolation">10</a>. Write $p_i=p_{\ell_i}$, and combine the two desired inequalities into the slacks $$F_i(s)=\sigma_iH_i(s)+\delta_iT_k(s),\qquad
 (\sigma_1,\sigma_2)=(-1,1),\qquad \delta_i=\mathbf1_{\{i=1\}}.$$ Thus we must prove $F_1,F_2\ge0$ on $[0,\infty)$. Exact interpolation already gives $$\label{eq:exact-slack-jets}
 F_i(m)=F_i'(m)=0\qquad(m\in\mathcal N).$$ The reference lists are $X_iq(k)$, extended by zero beyond $M=168$. Throughout this section, $H_i^{\mathrm{ref}}$ denotes the function formed from those lists with the *exact* direct and transformed spectral integrals. Only the finite coefficient rows of Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a> use quadrature.

The gaps in the full zero set have repeating lengths $1,2,1,3,2,3$. The signed half-gaps centered at the nodes through 88 therefore cover exactly $$[0,s_0],\qquad s_0=89.5.$$ The last is the right half-gap from 88 to the midpoint 89.5 of the gap to 91. Every signed half-length satisfies $1/2\le|y|\le3/2$. At the origin there is only the half-gap $[0,1/2]$, with $\nu=0$; at every positive center, $\nu=2$. Figure <a href="#fig:sign-junction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:sign-junction">1</a> records the junction with the tail.

<figure id="fig:sign-junction">

<figcaption>The finite inequalities cover the half-gaps through $s_{0}$. In the tail, 91 is a nearest node on $[89.5,92]$, then 93 on $[92,94.5]$, and so on. A double zero at the chosen node turns the uniform second-derivative estimate into a quadratic remainder bound.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Quotients after subtracting values and slopes

<a id="eq:deleted-slack"></a>
Recall the quotient $\mathcal D_{m,2}$ introduced before the finite polynomial rows in Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a>. For a twice continuously differentiable function $u$, Taylor’s formula gives $$\mathcal D_{m,2}u(s)
 =\frac{u(s)-u(m)-(s-m)u'(m)}{(s-m)^2}
 =\int_0^1(1-v)u''\bigl(m+v(s-m)\bigr)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dv.$$ The integral also gives the removable value at $s=m$. As before, $\mathcal D_{0,0}u=u$ at the origin. On a prescribed half-gap $s=m+yx$, $0\le x\le1$, the relevant quotient is $$\label{eq:deleted-slack}
 \mathcal D_{m,\nu}F_i(s)
 =\sigma_i\mathcal D_{m,\nu}H_i(s)
  +\delta_i\mathcal D_{m,\nu}T_k(s).$$ For $m>0$, <a href="#eq:exact-slack-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-slack-jets">[eq:exact-slack-jets]</a> makes this equal to $F_i(s)/(s-m)^2$ away from the center. The deletion is performed on each function before any approximation is made, so a nonzero constant or linear approximation error is never divided by $(s-m)^2$.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Moving from the exact lists to the reference lists.

<a id="eq:deleted-list-error"></a>
Their combined $\ell^1$ distance is less than $2\cdot10^{-8}$. A unit list has spectral variation at most 36, direct frequency modulus at most one, and transformed bounds $|\lambda|<3$, $|z|<3$. The transformed damping has positive exponent $\pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z$, so there is no exponential growth at nonnegative radii. Taylor’s integral formula and the derivative estimates of Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a> therefore give the following bound. Each $H_i-H_i^{\mathrm{ref}}$ contains a direct error from one list and a transformed error from the other. The $\ell^1$ norms of the two list differences add to less than $2\cdot10^{-8}$, so the larger of the two kernel bounds controls the sum: $$\begin{aligned}
 \sup_{s\text{ in the half-gap}}
 \left|\mathcal D_{m,\nu}(H_i-H_i^{\mathrm{ref}})(s)\right|
 &\le\frac{36}{\nu!}(2\cdot10^{-8})
       \max\{\pi^\nu,3(3\pi)^\nu\}\notag\\
 &<.00011.\label{eq:deleted-list-error}
\end{aligned}$$ For $\nu=0$, this is the zeroth-derivative estimate. For $\nu=2$, the factor $1/2$ is $\int_0^1(1-v)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dv$, and the last comparison is the scalar bound in <a href="#eq:scalar-error-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-error-budgets">[eq:scalar-error-budgets]</a>. No zero jet is required of the reference function; only the exact slack uses <a href="#eq:exact-slack-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-slack-jets">[eq:exact-slack-jets]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Replacing the reference quotient by its finite polynomial.

<a id="eq:half-gap-polynomial-error"></a>
The entire Taylor series gives, in both cases $\nu=0,2$, $$\sigma_i\mathcal D_{m,\nu}H_i^{\mathrm{ref}}(m+yx)
 =\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}
   \sigma_i\frac{y^r}{(r+\nu)!}
     (H_i^{\mathrm{ref}})^{(r+\nu)}(m)x^r.$$ The rows $l_{i,r}$ in <a href="#eq:bernstein-rows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bernstein-rows">[eq:bernstein-rows]</a> replace the included derivatives by quadrature. We claim $$\label{eq:half-gap-polynomial-error}
 \sup_{0\le x\le1}
 \left|\sigma_i\mathcal D_{m,\nu}H_i^{\mathrm{ref}}(m+yx)
       -\sum_{r=0}^{40}l_{i,r}q(k)x^r\right|<.000001.$$ For the included coefficients, Lemma <a href="#lem:quadrature-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quadrature-error">7</a> controls the scaled moment of order $r+\nu$. The row above has the additional multiplier $y^{-\nu}$, whose absolute value is at most 4. By <a href="#eq:reference-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-norm">[eq:reference-norm]</a>, the combined reference-list norm is less than $4.8<5$. Summing the 41 coefficient errors therefore gives at most $41\cdot4\cdot5\cdot10^{-31}$.

<a id="eq:half-gap-series-tail"></a>
For the omitted powers, put $Y=|y|$ and $l=41+\nu$. Their total is at most $$\label{eq:half-gap-series-tail}
 \frac{Y^{-\nu}36\cdot5}
 {l!\bigl(1-3\pi Y/(l+1)\bigr)}
 \left((\pi Y)^l+
 3\max_{1\le j\le6}
 e^{-\pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t_j)m}
       \bigl(\pi|z(t_{j-1})|Y\bigr)^l\right).$$ Indeed, each direct derivative contributes a factor $i\pi t$, and each transformed derivative a factor $i\pi z(t)$. On $[t_{j-1},t_j]$, both $\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)$ and $|z(t)|$ decrease. Thus the right endpoint maximizes $e^{-\pi\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z(t)m}$, while the left endpoint bounds the largest frequency modulus, including the endpoint atoms. For each fixed frequency, the ratio of successive terms in the absolute exponential series is at most $3\pi Y/(l+1)<1$. Summing this geometric majorant and then integrating against total variation gives the displayed denominator. The factor $36\cdot5$ bounds the combined spectral variation of the reference inputs.

The scalar calculation in Appendix <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">9</a> replaces the maximum in <a href="#eq:half-gap-series-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:half-gap-series-tail">[eq:half-gap-series-tail]</a> by the larger sum over all six intervals. For $$(m,Y,\nu)=(0,1/2,0),\quad(1,1,2),\quad(3,1,2),\quad(4,3/2,2),$$ it gives, respectively, upper bounds $1.8\cdot10^{-19}$, $10^{-11}$, $3\cdot10^{-16}$, and $2\cdot10^{-11}$, all below $10^{-8}$. These four cases cover every half-gap. The origin is the first case; the largest half-lengths at 1 and 3 are one; and every later center is at least 4 with half-length at most $3/2$. For fixed $\nu$, the expression decreases as $m$ increases or $Y$ decreases: its powers contain $Y^{l-\nu}=Y^{41}$, and the positive geometric denominator also improves when $Y$ decreases. Adding the included quadrature error proves <a href="#eq:half-gap-polynomial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:half-gap-polynomial-error">[eq:half-gap-polynomial-error]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Lower supports for every target regime

<a id="stmt-lem-exponential-support"></a>
<div id="lem:exponential-support" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Exponential supports). *Let $K,J$ be consecutive endpoints of the parameter partition in Section <a href="#sec:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite">3</a>, and let $K\le k\le J$, with no upper restriction when $J=\infty$. On every prescribed half-gap, the coefficients $a_r^*$ from <a href="#eq:target-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-support">[eq:target-support]</a> satisfy $$\mathcal D_{m,\nu}T_k(m+yx)
 \ge\sum_{r=0}^{40}a_r^*x^r-.000001\qquad(0\le x\le1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exponential-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exponential-support</summary>

*Proof.* At the origin, $0\le s\le1/2$, and $$\frac{\partial T_k(s)}{\partial k}
 =T_k(s)\left(1-s-\frac1k\right)>0$$ because $k\ge2.36>2$. Replacing $k$ by $K$ therefore lowers the target. The Taylor polynomial of $T_K(yx)$ through degree 40 has coefficients $(e^K/K)(-Ky)^r/r!$, exactly the first case in <a href="#eq:target-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-support">[eq:target-support]</a>.

At the first positive node, write $u=s-1$. The deleted quotient is $$\mathcal D_{1,2}T_k(1+u)=\frac{e^{-ku}-1+ku}{ku^2},$$ with value $k/2$ at $u=0$.
